Not doing, and robbing, producing, and so on.
For doing and the like, in reshaping deeds:
matchless, and talk,
bustle, and discarding and giving away.
At the end of a kind, its collection,
ubaganaka and so on.
The inanimate, the mindless, the kindless,
and likewise the mindless and so on.
Child and offspring.
Going together and going at one time.
By brahmins, crowds, and people,
a gathering put off becomes halanaṃ.
Battleground, battlefield, battlefront.
Side and back
are just pārśvaṃ and pṛṣṭhaṃ.
A cluster of villages, a crowd of people, a mass of smoke,
a set of nooses: each a collection.
A collection of thousands, a mass of flames,
a set of armor, and the Vedas and so on too.
From completing the sections and their divisions,
explanation is set forth as one wishes.
Distinguished by a bound continuum:
the tale and the full narration,
the fragmentary tale and the collection;
the principal and the others too are to be understood.
Section, division, chapter,
and boundaries marked by full division, without end.
Word, proclamation, and illumination.
Occasion in the feminine, and the masculine calf.
The text of Aṅga, which makes clear,
is also fully gathered in this place.
Separate verses, each in its own place,
from within mutual discourse.
Bound in sarga is the great poem;
the great embodied form is in drama.
The sages' treatise on all dharmā,
the scripture of remembrance, and the treatise.
Number, crest, and measure,
the two texts, two and three, the one with ten letters.
Inference and the one with scripture,
the reason, pervasion, the sign, and the proof.
Then cause and substantial cause,
condition and cooperating factor.
Reading and familiarity with it,
compendium and practice.
Concordant with the cause and resembling it,
kunda, the Pleiades, the teller of time.
Intention and conduct.
Wheel and maṇḍala.
śunḍanaṃ and moving.
kṣoraṃ is also making good.
The feathered, and whatever does that,
the supreme, and whatever torments.
By the rāmāyaṇa of Rāma:
proclaiming sound, bringing to exhaustion.
Entity, the meaning of a word, phenomenon,
sentient being, gone, and conduct.
Casting the aspect of the past,
what must be established, many letters.
Suchness and its instant,
word, and also "principal."
Boy and uncompounded,
obscuring and unsupported.
Whatever does, and doing individually,
each itself carried through to the end.
Thorough etymology, the subsequent manner,
together with sāntānaṃ: these three are alike.
Whatever is radiant, path,
principal itself, and light entering.
Then water, breadth, and place,
height, and "having form."
Wholly enjoying and wholly destroying,
wholly longing and wholly holding,
wholly correcting and wholly making,
wholly reviling and wholly covering.
Portion, birth, poison, weapon,
the one to be killed, and wild beasts.
Fitting, appearance, destruction,
thick, and turned to dregs are expressed.
lāṭi, petals, heads,
place, district, settled abode.
Portion, near word, scent and food,
binding and searching,
the property of the Buddha and the wealth of a stupa,
the property of sentient beings, and kin.
By the number six, in the word for cattle,
it is taken as "six cows."
Two kinds, dispute, and form,
doing, the migrator, joy,
virtue, misdeed, written on the heart.
Thought, mind, and done in the proper way.
Turning the wheel, and weeding,
certain destruction and not going.
Cluster of villages, village, and sweeping,
the chaffy, wealth, and the joyful.
Merchant and merchandise,
attending closely and casting aside.
ruṇḍa, body, binding, vessel, and abode.
Site, livelihood, and drink,
heart, and skin, are held to be down.
ala, a hundred, and the place of bowing:
here they are not established thus.
Deceiving, and the meadow,
garland, row, and entering in rows.
Holding all, and the water vessel.
Stupa, and the gate of the guide.
Direct perception, and seeing the object clearly.
Hero, subduing, and overpowering.
Then extolling and undertaking,
and also "turned toward it."
Born of one's own longing, and bowing,
born of agitation, and as connection.
siṇo, the water's baraṇḍa,
pakura, and abiding truly.
Protecting all, merchandise, bowing,
golden vase, boundary, and the nosed one,
full, accomplishing, supreme bowing.
Destroyer and aṣṭaka.
Elephant, drum, and great bath,
Great Brahmā, hearing, and porridge.
ābalyaka utkaṇṭha.
Minister, intermediary, two meetings.
Frog and cow killer.
Food and making, tarkuṭa.
Day, macukaṃ, neuter.
māmā jyiti belongs to horses.

This completes the mixed section of Clear Light on All Things, also called Pearl Garland, composed by the master poet, the paṇḍita, the glorious Śrīdharasena.

This concludes the translation. The following are translator's notes.

This is a Sanskrit synonym lexicon (in the tradition of Amarasiṃha's Amarakośa, which the author names as his model); most entries are Tibetan calques of Sanskrit synonyms, rendered here by their literal sense, and many Sanskrit words transcribed in Tibetan letters are visibly corrupt in the Tibetan, so they are given as spelled. The Tibetan has small displacements: the count "thirty names" for heaven and "fifty-two names" for the gods stand inside the lists rather than at their ends, and are kept where they occur. The opening verses are very compressed and in places obscure (notably the end of the first verse, the first line of the second, and the close of the sixth); they are rendered as literally as sense allows. Munisena and Śrīdhara (the author Śrīdharasena) are identified from the Tibetan thub pa'i sde and dpal 'dzin.  This is a synonym lexicon whose Tibetan is interrupted by interlinear headings ("N names for X") and Sanskrit glosses that have been copied into the middle of verse lines (for example "Twenty-three names for Avalokiteśvara" splits "Of Many Forms," and "Eight names for Marīcī" splits "the Youth and the Crest-Bearer"); they are translated where they stand. Several running counts do not match the items listed (thirteen for the Avalokiteśvara list announced as twenty-three; three after a pair of names); the numbers are given as written. Uncertain renderings: "the One of Ten, the Twelve-Eyed"; "Lord of Kings" (literally "lord of earth-sustainers"); "The Devadattas" in the Buddha list; "His son, shown as one of perfect enjoyment"; "The crest: Devadatta"; "Delighted by the Maiden"; "Lion-Fierce"; "the Beautiful Destroyer"; and the closing three epithets, which seem to begin a list completed in the next piece.  As in the preceding pieces, interlinear headings and Sanskrit glosses have been copied into the verse lines; they are translated where they stand, and running counts are given as written even where they do not fit. "Six names for the monk" reads "dge slong la med drug", where med is evidently a slip for ming, "name". In "His father is Vasudeva", nor lha (elsewhere in this text the Vasu gods) here names Kṛṣṇa's father, so Vasudeva is used. Many epithets in the Viṣṇu and Balarāma lists cannot be matched with confidence to a Sanskrit original and are rendered literally, notably "the Hand-Like", "the Ivory-Born", "Son of the Woman Dull of Hearing", "the Half-Text", "He of the Net amid the Rubbish Heaps", "the Ripened, the Faithful, Yama", "Hand of the Ender", "Maker of the Lord of the Earth" and "the Unclimbing". "The Delighter" after the bow probably stands for Viṣṇu's sword Nandaka.  As before, interlinear headings and glosses are embedded in the verse lines and are translated where they stand; running counts are given as written even where they disagree with the headings (the Kāma list is headed eighty-nine but closes with twenty-nine). In the Śiva list, ʼphrog byed, used of Viṣṇu above as Hari, is rendered Hara, its sense when applied to Śiva, and rnam ʼjigs is rendered "the Terrifying", since here it is an epithet of Śiva rather than the name Vibhīṣaṇa. The lines "This is judged to be unconnected" and "from the eight the All-Destroyer, worthy, worthy, the Victor is also he" appear to be scribal or interlinear remarks copied into the text. A number of epithets could not be identified with a Sanskrit original and are rendered literally, among them "the Mountain-Dull", "Ground of the Withered Body", "He Named with I", "the Fish-Jointed", "the One Who Moves from Where", "the Rotting-Bodied" and "He Who Speaks at the Boundaries". The verse giving the lunar mansion, night and moment of the Destroyer of Beings' birth is rendered literally; its names are uncertain.  As in the earlier pieces, interlinear headings (such as "Ninety-three names of Īśvara's wife" and "Seventeen names for Gaṇapati") and stray words have been copied into the verse; they are translated where they stand. In the Gaṇapati verse the words "the Aging Lady" and "Head of the Fierce Lady" fall between the syllables of tshogs ... bdag (Gaṇapati), so they are marked as standing between. Grol bum is taken as a variant of grul bum, kumbhāṇḍa. In the list of the Goddess's names, dga' ma (elsewhere Rati) and 'phrog byed (elsewhere Hari) are taken as epithets ("the Delighting Lady", "Graceful Lady of Hari"), and dbang phyug, Īśvara, appears in the feminine, Īśvarī. Several epithets are rendered literally because their Sanskrit originals are unclear, among them "Lord of Those Who Fly Upward", "Cutter of the Good", "the Piler", "the Threadless Lady", "the Escort" and "Garland of Pretence".  As throughout this lexicon, interlinear headings (such as "One hundred and twelve names of Śakra" and "Names of Śakra's horse") are embedded in the verse lines and translated where they stand; the running counts are given as written. Ḥphrog byed, used of Indra here, is kept as Hari (Sanskrit hari also means a bay horse, Indra's steed). The names in the Nārada and Aśvin lists are largely literal renderings whose Sanskrit originals are uncertain (for example "Son of the Man-Giver", "the Mother's Mark", "the Aśvinī Dog"). "Names for nectar" falls inside the list of divine trees and seems to be a displaced heading for the preceding verse.  This is a synonym lexicon whose transmitted text is interlarded with interlinear glosses and Sanskrit forms in Tibetan letters (for example "for fire there are one hundred and seven names," "for rākṣasas in general there are forty-two names," and the fragments inside the rākṣasa entry); these have been translated where they stand rather than removed. Several epithets of Agni, Yama and Rāvaṇa could not be securely matched to their Sanskrit originals and are rendered by meaning (e.g. Lhaji, the Slow-Holder, the Reckoner, Golden Sport). "Rider of the Goat" reads the sequence sgrub pa ra la zhon as Accomplisher plus Chāgaratha. In the list of flames, phur bu renders Sanskrit kīla, "flame," not a ritual dagger. The bracketed counts follow the text even where they do not match the number of items listed.  The text is a synonym lexicon interspersed with interlinear glosses and with Sanskrit forms and grammatical remarks written in Tibetan letters, notably in the section on the directions; these remarks are translated as they stand, and their grammatical sense is at points uncertain. Several epithets of Varuṇa, Vāyu and Kubera could not be securely identified and are rendered by meaning. In the list of Kubera's names, ལུས་ངན ("Ugly Body") is the standard rendering of Kubera and is given so. In the list of the sky's names, སྒྲ་སྒྲོགས is rendered "the Resounding" rather than as the name Rāvaṇa, since it is an epithet of space here. The identifications of the elephants and cow-elephants of the directions follow the classical lists where the Tibetan clearly matches them and are otherwise rendered literally.  The passage belongs to a synonym lexicon and preserves interlinear glosses and Sanskrit forms in Tibetan letters; these are translated where they stand, and many of the epithets of cloud, lightning, hail, fog and moon are rendered by meaning because their Sanskrit originals cannot be confidently identified. In the list of the moon's names, དབང་པོ renders Sanskrit indu, a name of the moon, and is given as Indu rather than Indra. The final verses list names of Dhruva, the pole star, and the mothers of various beings; the gloss "names for cold" stands misplaced in the text and is kept as it stands. The counts follow the text even where they do not match the items given.  The passage is a synonym lexicon with interlinear glosses and Sanskrit forms written in Tibetan letters; these are translated where they stand. It opens with the names of the sage Agastya (the star Canopus), the Pot-Born who shrank the ocean, then gives the lunar mansions with their presiding deities, which are identified by their standard Sanskrit names, the planets, the seven sages, the zodiacal houses and the names of the sun. Many epithets, particularly of Agastya and the sun, could not be securely matched to Sanskrit and are rendered by meaning. The counts follow the text even where they do not match the items given.  The passage belongs to a synonym lexicon interspersed with interlinear glosses (such as "names of the night" and "names for the new moon") and with Sanskrit forms in Tibetan letters; the glosses are translated where they stand. Many epithets of the sun's consorts, horses and charioteer (Aruṇa, the dawn) and of the night are rendered by meaning, since their Sanskrit originals cannot be securely identified. The months are given by their Sanskrit names, matched to the lunar mansions named in the Tibetan. The counts follow the text even where they do not match the items listed.  This lexicon (a synonym dictionary of the Amarakośa type) has interlinear glosses that have entered the running text, such as "ten names for summer," "names of evil," "names of joy," "names of the self," "names of the day of the fortnight" and "names of mid-autumn." They are translated where they stand, and some sit in places where they do not fit the list they interrupt. The numerals after each group give the count of synonyms and are kept as given, even where they do not match the items. "Sak-born" (སག་སྐྱེས) could not be identified and is rendered literally. "The Head-born" probably refers to the months counted from Mārgaśīrṣa ("the Head"). In the list of auspicious words, ཞི་བ stands for Sanskrit śiva, "auspicious," and not the god. In "the festival of Smara," དྲན་པ means the god of love, not mindfulness. In "the retentive," གཟུངས means mental retention, not a dhāraṇī. "The clove" (ལི་ཤུ) in the list of words for mind is odd but is translated as written. The cosmological verses on the days of the hungry ghosts, gods, Brahmā and Manu are broken up by glosses and are translated as they stand.  As in the earlier pieces, this is a synonym lexicon with counts after each group, and interlinear glosses ("names of fragrance," "names of white," "black," "names of colors") have entered the text. They are translated where they stand. In the list for liberation, ཞི་བ (śiva, "peaceful") and ཚངས་པ (brahman) are synonyms for liberation, not the gods Śiva and Brahmā. In "tradition (smṛti)," དྲན་པ is the body of remembered law, not Smara or mindfulness. In the list of colors, ཀ་དྲུ is the color term kadru, "tawny," not the goddess Kadrū. The line "for the stringing of sounds it is gumbyane" (སྒྲ་ཡི་མ་ལ་ནི་གུ་མྦྱ་ནེ) is uncertain. Some taste and smell terms ("the abundant," "the heart-descending smell") are rendered literally because they could not be identified.  This is a synonym lexicon, and the numbers give the count of each group as the text has them. For the seven musical notes the Tibetan uses standard renderings (འཁོར་ཉན, ཁྱུ་མཆོག, དྲི་འཛིན, དྲུག་སྐྱེས, བར་མ, བློ་གསལ, ལྔ་པ). These are given as the Sanskrit niṣāda, ṛṣabha, gāndhāra, ṣaḍja, madhyama, dhaivata and pañcama. ཟླ་བ་ཅན, "moon-bearer," is taken as the peacock (candrakin). In "a deep sound is mantra," སྔགས most likely renders Sanskrit mandra, the low register, and was understood as mantra. A stray numeral "1" appears in the line on pañcama and is kept as "one." "Black" (ནག) before the reproach list looks like an interlinear gloss.  As before, the numbers give the count of terms in each group as the text has them, and some do not match. The stray numeral in "the talker and the dancer, one" (གར ༡ པ) is kept as it stands. In the list of forms of address used in drama, "consecrated" (དབང་བསྐུར་བ) means a queen's royal consecration, not a tantric empowerment. The ten sentiments (rasa) include two terms, མཉེས་པ "the delightful" and ཤིན་པ "the intense," that could not be identified with confidence and are rendered literally. "Samādhi" among the divisions of the erotic sentiment is translated as written. In the list of terrible things, འཇིགས་བྱེད is the adjective "terrifying," not the name Vibhīṣaṇa. The phrase "aprahmaṇya is what is not to be done" (ཨ་པྲ་ཧྨ་ཎྱ་པ་སང་བྱ་མིན) is uncertain.  This piece holds the colophon that closes the section on heaven, naming the text and its author, Śrīdharasena (དཔལ་འཛིན་སྡེ). It then opens the section on the underworld. The line "day and night, year, instant, laughter and festivals: as for these" breaks off before the colophon, and probably once gave their grammatical gender. ནོར་རྒྱས་བུ is taken as Vāsuki, by itself and as "king of serpents," following the parallel in the Amarakośa. Several serpent names are rendered literally because they could not be identified with confidence: "Horse-spittle," "the Brahmā serpent," "the Resonant," and "the Ass-haired one." ཀུ་དྲུ is read as Kadrū, mother of the nāgas. In "near fever," རིམས is not preceded by a word of rank or row, so it is read as fever.  This is a synonym lexicon, so many entries are rare Sanskrit words merely transliterated in the Tibetan; these are given romanized as the Tibetan spells them. Several Tibetan calques could not be identified with confidence and are translated literally (for example the hollow-lier, the softener, the one born of the śagam, the bliss-binder). ལྟོ་འཕྱེ is rendered mahoraga per the house glossary, though in a list of snake synonyms it corresponds to Sanskrit uraga. བདེ་མཆོག་བྱེད in the list of words for water is taken as śaṃvara, a Sanskrit word for water, not the deity Cakrasaṃvara; likewise བདེ་བྱེད there is the common noun "bliss-maker", not the name Śaṃkara. ཁྱབ་འཇུག རྐང་པ འཇིགས་བྱེད་སྐྱེས is read as Viṣṇupadī and Bhīṣmasū, epithets of the Gaṅgā.  This piece continues the lexicon's lists of words for water, banks, boats, fishing, fish and aquatic creatures. Many entries are rare Sanskrit words only transliterated in the Tibetan and are given romanized as spelled; many Tibetan calques could not be matched to a Sanskrit term with confidence and are translated literally. ཤ་ཟ here is a fish name, rendered "the flesh-eater" rather than as the spirit class piśāca. ཕུར་བུ here is read as a word for a fishing implement ("the peg"), not the planet. བདེ་བྱེད in "the donkey's bliss-maker" is a common noun, not the name Śaṃkara.  This piece closes the lexicon's lists of aquatic creatures, wells, ponds and lotuses, and ends the first chapter. Many entries are Sanskrit words only transliterated in the Tibetan and are given romanized as spelled; Tibetan calques that could not be matched with confidence are translated literally, and two opaque ones (ཆུ་ཡི་རྔམ, ཆུའི་ལེན་བུ) are kept as "ngam" and "lenbu". གཞན་མཐའ་ཡས here means "the others are endless", not the name Ananta. འཕྲོག་བྱེད in the crab and frog lists is taken as the Sanskrit word hari, used here as a common noun. ཐུན་མཚམས is "twilight", not a ritual term.  This piece opens the second chapter of the lexicon, on the earth, its kinds of ground, continents and lands. Many entries are Sanskrit words only transliterated in the Tibetan and are romanized as spelled; Tibetan calques of place names that could not be identified with confidence are translated literally. སྒྲ་ངན་ཞིང is read as Kurukṣetra and སྒྲ་མི་སྙན as Uttarakuru, standard Tibetan renderings. In the list of earth names, མཐའ་ཡས is the epithet Anantā, and རི་ཡི་ཕུར་བུ་མ is "pegged by mountains", not the planet Jupiter. སྐྱ་སྣར་བུ is read as Pāṭaliputra, མཉམ་ཡོད as Śrāvastī, and ལུས་འཕགས as Videha.  This piece finishes the lexicon's section on the earth, with roads and distances, and begins the section on towns and buildings. Many entries are Sanskrit words only transliterated in the Tibetan and are romanized as spelled; calques that could not be matched with confidence are translated literally. འཕྲོག་བྱེད་ཟླ་གྲོང is read as Hariścandrapura, a name for the illusory city of the gandharvas. ཕུར་བུ here is "peg", not the planet, and དབྱུག་གུ is the door bar, not a unit of time.  This is a synonym lexicon: each line lists words for one thing, and the numerals give the count of synonyms as the text states them; the counts do not always match the items and are kept as written. Sanskrit words merely transliterated in the Tibetan are given in romanized form as spelled. The phrases "thirty-one names for mountain" and "twenty-eight names for tree" appear to be interlinear glosses absorbed into the text; they are translated where they stand. In "the line" the text reads mtshar ("wonder"), evidently for tshar ("row, line"), which fits the list of words for "row". The words rendered "jubo", "tun", "the mountain's outpouring" (bud) and "the shelter it shows" (grag) are uncertain; the first two are left in their Tibetan form. The slow-flowing mountain may be Mandara, but this is not certain.  A synonym lexicon: each run of words names one thing, and the numerals are the counts the text gives, kept as written even where they do not match the items. Words merely transliterated from Sanskrit are given romanized as spelled. Several phrases ("Names of the tree of awakening...", "These are names of the mango", "Names of the pomegranate", "Names of the campaka, the nāga and gold", "Names of the heap...", "Names of the very sour great juice") look like interlinear glosses absorbed into the text; they are translated where they stand. In "sahākara" the gloss divides the transliterated word sa-hākara (the fragrant mango); it is read as one word. Some words are uncertain and are kept in Tibetan form (khadzur, rebrago, butshaba, sanma, melebrgan, tok, thang gi kro). "Kubera's eye" renders the Tibetan for "ugly body" plus "eye", the standard rendering of Kuberākṣī, a name of the pāṭalī. In the citron list, "compassion" renders a word that stands for the Sanskrit plant name karuṇa.  A synonym lexicon: the numerals are the counts the text gives, kept as written even where they do not match the items, and words merely transliterated from Sanskrit are given romanized as spelled. Phrases such as "names of the grape", "names of karavīra", "names of devadāru" and "names of the coral tree" look like interlinear glosses absorbed into the text; they are translated where they stand. The coconut list ("This man-trunk ... the sap-producer") is especially disordered, with glosses and broken words mixed in; it is rendered literally. Some words are uncertain and kept in Tibetan form (starbu, dra ba mi pa, drago, shingsar, mumen, byaru, soka). "Kin-holder" and the like render rtsa lag, "kin", reflecting the Sanskrit bandhu in bandhūka. "The ender of the mango" renders the words for "mango" and "ender", here a plant name, not Death.  A synonym lexicon: the numerals are the counts the text gives, kept as written even where they do not match the items, and words merely transliterated from Sanskrit are given romanized as spelled. Phrases such as "names of the lac tree", "names of the shingdru", "names of the lungthang tree" and "names of the aguru tree" look like interlinear glosses absorbed into the text; they are translated where they stand. Some words are uncertain and kept in Tibetan form (shingdru, lungthang, bha sidrub, chaba, dilcib, kyurura, rabtura, sumtshan, biu). The lists here describe, in order, among others the drumstick tree, soapnut, saptaparṇa, arjuna, birch, wood-apple, udumbara, śirīṣa, aguru, the three myrobalans, nimba, silk-cotton, cassia, kuṭaja, vetasa, lodhra, khadira and castor. "Supreme bliss of crops" translates the words bde mchog literally in a plant name; it does not refer to the deity Cakrasaṃvara. The last line breaks off mid-entry, as the piece does.  A synonym lexicon of plants: the numerals are the counts the text gives, kept as written even where they do not match the items; words merely transliterated from Sanskrit are given romanized as spelled; the section turns from verse to prose partway through, and the translation follows it. Some words are uncertain and kept in Tibetan form (shor, spal, ebarma, brnas, rtigla, srisi, shudag, gnasālā). "The lord of cattle" (Paśupati) and "Indra" render the Tibetan in plant names here, not the gods themselves. "The Videha-born" renders the plant name Vaidehī (long pepper).  This is a lexical list of plant synonyms; Tibetan calques are rendered by their meaning and Sanskrit words written in Tibetan letters are romanized as spelled. Several items are uncertain identifications: ལེ་བརྒན is taken as madder because the following synonyms (jiṅgī, the frog-leaved, the bright-leaved) are those of mañjiṣṭhā; མོན here renders Kirāta (kirātatikta). The numeral after the viḍaṅga list is written ༩༨ ("ninety-eight") though ten items are listed; it is translated as written. Item counts elsewhere often do not match the items given and are kept as the text has them. ཆེར་མ is read as ཚེར་མ ("thorn"). Obscure Tibetan calques such as ཚོར་གཟེམ and མེ་དྲུ are rendered phonetically or literally. The following is a note about this translation.

This is an AI-generated translation from the AI Stupa project. It was produced by a careful, doctrine-aware process aiming at a high degree of accuracy, but it has not been reviewed by a human translator and may contain errors. It is offered as a provisional translation for those texts of the Tibetan Buddhist canon that have not yet received an authoritative human translation. Where a published translation exists — above all from 84000: Translating the Words of the Buddha (84000.co) — that translation is definitive and should be preferred; please consult it instead. These translations are released into the public domain (CC0) in the spirit of spreading the Dharma to all corners of the earth.

Just as the Bodhi tree now standing at Bodh Gayā neither is nor is not the same tree beneath which the Buddha attained awakening, so too this stupa is intended as the next generation of stupa — dependently arising according to the conditions of its environment, and ceaselessly proclaiming the Dharma for the benefit of all sentient beings.

May whoever encounters these words derive great benefit.
